DNA on the Shroud of Turin reveals centuries of hidden secrets

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Scientists have uncovered a remarkable collection of DNA hidden within the Shroud of Turin, revealing a surprisingly complex biological history behind one of the world's most mysterious and controversial religious artifacts. The genetic evidence includes traces of humans, bacteria, fungi, plants, animals, and even Mediterranean red coral, offering new clues about the environments and people that have come into contact with the famous cloth over the centuries.

Published in Scientific Reports, the study used advanced DNA sequencing technology to examine samples collected from the Shroud in 1978. For the first time, researchers applied a sophisticated technique (rigorous PCR-free next-generation metagenomic sequencing) to these officially collected samples, allowing them to identify genetic material preserved within the linen fibers in unprecedented detail.

Unlike traditional DNA testing, which often focuses on genetic material from a single organism, metagenomic sequencing can detect DNA from many different species within the same sample. This makes it particularly useful for studying historical objects that have been handled, stored, and exposed to changing environments over long periods.

By examining this genetic material, the international research team assembled what they describe as the most comprehensive picture yet of the biological traces accumulated on the Shroud throughout its history.

Human DNA and a Hidden World of Microorganisms

Among the discoveries were multiple lineages of human mitochondrial DNA, suggesting that genetic material from different human populations has accumulated on the cloth. Mitochondrial DNA is a small portion of our genetic material that is inherited through the maternal line, making it especially useful for studying ancestry and population history.

The researchers also identified an unexpectedly diverse microbiome, the collection of microscopic organisms associated with a particular environment or object. These included bacteria commonly found on human skin, various fungi, and microorganisms adapted to living in salty environments.

Some of these organisms belong to a group known as archaea, which are single-celled microorganisms distinct from bacteria. Certain archaea can thrive in extremely salty conditions that would be difficult for many other forms of life to tolerate.

Together, these findings reveal that the Shroud carries a complicated biological record shaped by repeated human contact and exposure to different environmental conditions.

DNA From Plants, Animals, and Mediterranean Coral

The discoveries extended far beyond human DNA and microorganisms. Scientists also detected genetic traces from numerous plant and animal species, adding another layer to the Shroud's biological history.

Plant DNA included cultivated wheat, carrots, maize, bananas, and peanuts. Genetic material from domesticated animals was also present, including cattle, pigs, chickens, dogs, and cats.

One particularly unusual finding was DNA associated with Mediterranean red coral.

The wide variety of biological material suggests that the cloth has encountered a diverse range of living organisms and environmental influences. These genetic traces may reflect its long history of handling, storage, and movement through different geographical regions.

Importantly, the presence of DNA from a particular species does not establish when that material reached the cloth. Some traces could have accumulated relatively recently, while others may reflect much older interactions. This makes the findings valuable for reconstructing the Shroud's environmental history, but also highlights the difficulty of interpreting DNA recovered from an object that has been exposed to human activity for centuries.

Can DNA Solve the Mystery of the Shroud of Turin?

Despite the remarkable diversity of genetic material, the researchers emphasize that their findings cannot establish the Shroud's age or authenticity.

The Shroud of Turin is a linen cloth bearing the faint image of a human figure and has long been associated by some believers with the burial of Jesus. Its origins have been debated for generations, making it one of the most extensively investigated historical and religious artifacts in the world.

Because the cloth has been touched and exposed to different environments over many centuries, its DNA represents a mixture of biological material accumulated at different times. This makes it impossible to reliably separate any original DNA that might have been associated with the fabric from the many genetic traces deposited afterward.

Instead, the research demonstrates how modern forensic genomics and proteomics can reveal information that traditional methods might miss. Forensic genomics uses genetic evidence to investigate biological origins and interactions, while proteomics examines proteins, which can also preserve valuable clues about biological materials.

Together, these approaches offer powerful new ways to investigate how historical objects have been preserved, handled, and exposed to different environments.

Modern DNA Technology Reveals New Historical Clues

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, contributed to the international research collaboration. She emphasized that advances in forensic technology are making it possible to recover information from historical samples collected decades earlier.

Professor Noemi Procopio, Professor of Forensic Science at the University of Lancashire, said: "The Shroud of Turin continues to fascinate historians, scientists and the public, and we're thrilled to reveal new information about the biological complexity preserved within samples collected almost 50 years ago by applying new cutting-edge forensic DNA technologies.

"The Shroud represents a rich archive of genetic information that has accumulated over centuries of human interaction and environmental exposure. While the DNA evidence cannot answer all the questions about the age or authenticity of the cloth, it provides novel insights into its biological history and demonstrates how advances in forensic science can unlock new information from historical artifacts."

Materials provided by University of Lancashire. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

NASA reveals bold new plan to return astronauts to the Moon in 2028

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

NASA has revealed new details about its accelerated effort to return American astronauts to the Moon in 2028. The updated strategy includes testing two competing lunar landers, streamlining rocket preparations, and introducing a lighter spacesuit to help make the first moonwalks possible sooner.

During an October 23 briefing, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman and other agency leaders outlined the next steps for the Artemis program. The revised approach centers on two major missions: Artemis III, a test flight scheduled for 2027, and Artemis IV, which is intended to land astronauts on the lunar surface in 2028.

The announcement builds on priorities introduced earlier this year through NASA's Ignition initiative. Agency officials also highlighted recent progress in spacecraft development, mission preparations, and partnerships with private aerospace companies. These efforts are intended to reduce technical risks, accelerate the schedule, and establish the foundation for a lasting American presence on the Moon.

"This is a very energized NASA, and these updates are only a small part of that story. We are bringing extreme focus to the mission to ensure delivery of world-changing outcomes as quickly, safely, affordably, and successfully as possible," said Isaacman. "NASA is driving outcomes to make Moon missions a reality, and we're not waiting for others to bring their plans to us -- the campaign must move with urgency as strategic competitors pursue a permanent presence beyond Earth."

Artemis III Will Test Two Lunar Landers in Earth Orbit

Before attempting another crewed Moon landing, NASA plans to conduct a major rehearsal in Earth orbit. The Artemis III mission will bring together NASA's Orion spacecraft and experimental lunar landers developed by SpaceX and Blue Origin.

Four astronauts will travel aboard Orion, launching on NASA's powerful SLS (Space Launch System) rocket. During the planned 14-day mission, the crew will practice rendezvous and docking procedures with both companies' lander test vehicles. Rendezvous involves guiding two spacecraft to meet in orbit, while docking allows them to connect so astronauts can move between vehicles.

These demonstrations are designed to verify important spacecraft capabilities before Artemis IV attempts to carry astronauts to the Moon's surface.

The tests will take place in a carefully planned sequence. Blue Origin will first launch a test version of its Blue Moon lander into Earth orbit. NASA will then send Orion and its four astronauts into space aboard the SLS rocket, where Orion will rendezvous and dock with the Blue Moon vehicle.

Next, SpaceX will launch a test version of its Version 3 Starship lunar lander. Orion will separate from Blue Moon and perform another docking maneuver, this time with Starship. By testing both systems during a single crewed mission, NASA aims to gather essential operational experience while reducing uncertainties before the 2028 landing attempt.

NASA Cuts Rocket Preparation Times and Costs

The new mission details follow other recent Artemis progress reports. According to NASA, the hardware needed for Artemis III remains on schedule, and improvements in processing have reduced preparation timelines by approximately 50%.

The agency also reported a significant engineering achievement involving a critical rocket fuel-line component. Rather than relying on an outside supplier, NASA engineers redesigned, manufactured, and tested the component themselves in just five months. The work cost roughly one-tenth of an earlier estimate from an external vendor.

"NASA should be expected to do this kind of work, and we have some of the best technical knowledge in the world on rocket hardware and operations to do it," said Jeremy Parsons, the agency's Artemis program manager. "It's an example of how we are rebuilding our core competencies and developing centers of excellence in technical functions instead of continuing to outsource expertise."

The improvements reflect NASA's broader effort to strengthen its internal engineering capabilities, shorten development schedules, and reduce the cost of preparing missions for launch.

SpaceX and Blue Origin Offer Different Paths to the Moon

For Artemis IV in 2028, NASA intends to launch astronauts aboard Orion using the SLS rocket from Kennedy Space Center in Florida. Once in space, the crew will rely on a lunar landing system developed by either SpaceX or Blue Origin to reach the Moon's surface.

Both companies have revised their original mission designs to make operations simpler, more efficient, and more adaptable. Although they share the same objective, their approaches to transporting astronauts to the lunar surface differ considerably.

SpaceX plans to use its Starship spacecraft, which will take on additional propellant while orbiting Earth. After refueling, Starship will meet Orion and carry the spacecraft toward the Moon. Refueling in orbit is an important part of SpaceX's strategy because it allows the vehicle to receive the propellant needed for its journey after leaving the launchpad.

Blue Origin is developing a different approach using its Blue Moon Mark 2 lander. Its plan calls for additional propulsion stages in Earth orbit to send the lander toward the Moon. The lander will then meet Orion in a relatively low lunar orbit before carrying astronauts down to the surface.

Both companies are working toward having their systems ready to support a crewed Moon landing in 2028. Maintaining two potential landing systems gives NASA greater flexibility as development and testing continue.

NASA Explores New Rocket Options for Artemis IV

NASA is also considering two possible upper stages for the SLS rocket used on Artemis IV. An upper stage is the portion of a rocket that provides additional propulsion after the earlier stages have completed their work, helping send a spacecraft toward its destination.

One option is the interim cryogenic propulsion stage already used by SLS. The other is an extended version of United Launch Alliance's Centaur V upper stage, which would hold additional propellant.

By developing both possibilities, NASA hopes to preserve flexibility in its launch plans and avoid becoming dependent on a single technical solution. Having alternatives could help the agency respond to engineering challenges without jeopardizing the broader Moon landing schedule.

A New Lightweight Spacesuit Could Speed Up Moonwalks

Another important change involves the spacesuits astronauts will wear while exploring the lunar surface.

NASA is working with Axiom Space on a second, lighter design known as the "Sortie suit." The new suit is intended specifically for the first Moon surface excursions and weighs approximately half as much as Axiom's larger primary spacesuit.

The lighter design could simplify preparations for the earliest landing missions and help astronauts begin exploring the lunar surface sooner.

However, the Sortie suit is not intended to replace the primary design. Axiom's more capable suit will remain part of NASA's plans for future missions that require longer periods outside the spacecraft and more demanding surface activities.

Building a Permanent Human Presence on the Moon

NASA's ambitions extend well beyond returning astronauts to the lunar surface for a single mission.

As the Artemis program advances, the agency intends to send crews on increasingly challenging expeditions near the Moon's South Pole. This region is an important focus of future lunar exploration, and the missions are designed to expand how much of the Moon humans can investigate directly.

NASA describes the effort as part of a new Golden Age of innovation and exploration. The agency expects its lunar missions to support scientific discoveries, generate economic benefits, and encourage the development of technologies needed for more ambitious space exploration.

Ultimately, the goal is to establish an enduring human presence on the Moon rather than repeat the brief visits of earlier lunar expeditions. The experience gained from operating spacecraft, supporting astronauts, and carrying out increasingly complex missions on the lunar surface will also help NASA prepare for another historic objective: sending the first astronauts to Mars.

Materials provided by NASA. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

The climate case for AI has not been made

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

AI leaders claim their tech will fix the climate. The evidence mostly points in the opposite direction so far.

This week, as countries meet in Antalya, Turkey for the United Nations climate conference, one topic likely to dominate the conversation is artificial intelligence. The host has already called on countries to commit to ensuring that AI is designed, powered, and deployed in ways that support climate action.

But so far, industries around the world aren’t doing nearly enough to harness the technology to actually drive down the greenhouse-gas emissions heating up the planet, despite some very optimistic claims from AI companies.

In 2024, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman declared that AI would unleash “astounding triumphs,” including “fixing the climate.” The year before, in a 2023 op-ed, the head of sustainability at Google and her coauthor wrote that AI could reduce global emissions 5% to 10% by 2030.

Some academic researchers have examined these AI-related climate claims. A peer-reviewed study published last year, led by researchers at the London School of Economics’ Grantham Research Institute and the firm Systemiq, estimated that AI could help cut global emissions by the equivalent of 3.2 to 5.4 billion tons of carbon dioxide annually by 2035—roughly on par with the US’s annual energy-related emissions on the high end, even after subtracting AI’s own energy footprint. Those potential gains are mostly concentrated in three sectors: power, meat and dairy, and vehicles.

But projections like these are only as good as our willingness to test them against present-day reality. So is there evidence that the largest companies in these three sectors are beginning to integrate AI in line with the study’s projections, and if so, do the results match expectations? Or is the 2035 number still mostly aspirational?

Companies across these sectors have announced plenty of initial efforts to incorporate AI into various business practices. But an AI-assisted review of public information from significant market actors in each sector, conducted and reviewed by the second author of this piece, found that few have announced they’re pursuing the specific emissions-reducing routes that informed the projections in the study—and fewer still claim they’ve used them to achieve meaningful climate progress.

The power sector is where the earlier study’s underlying math is most concrete: AI-driven forecasting of supply and demand could increase the amount of renewable electricity that reaches the grid from already-built solar and wind facilities by up to 20%, which could prevent an estimated 1.8 billion tons of emissions a year.

Yet of the grid operators reviewed in China, the United States, and Europe, only one deployed a known meaningful, working AI program of this sort, according to the analysis. A partnership between the UK’s National Grid ESO and Open Climate Fix halved the grid operator’s solar-forecast error (the difference between the amount of incoming sunlight predicted and the amount that actually reaches solar panels), avoiding hundreds of thousands of tons of carbon dioxide a year. Beyond this example in the UK, though, many other grid operators in the EU, the US, and China had little to show in the public record in terms of the AI integration envisioned by the study.

The meat and dairy sector offers the starkest contrast between technology readiness and market reality. To achieve the 2035 emission reductions anticipated in the earlier study, AI would need to close the taste and cost gap between traditional proteins and alternative ones, such as lab-grown or plant-based burgers and milk substitutes.

In one of the clearest efforts to achieve this, the AI and food tech company NotCo recently formed a joint venture with Kraft Heinz using its platform Giuseppe, which analyzes plant molecular data to pinpoint combinations replicating the taste, texture, and smell of animal products.

But few other alternative-protein companies had such a deeply integrated way of using AI in product development, the AI-assisted review suggests. Meanwhile, the sector seems to be facing more fundamental problems, including stubbornly slow mainstream consumer adoption and declining funding levels. Most notably, venture capital flowing into cultivated meat fell from a $989 million peak in 2021 to around $83 million last year.

The transportation sector offers some hopeful examples of the type of AI integration necessary to achieve the study’s projections, particularly among battery manufacturers and ride-sharing platforms. CATL—which controls roughly 40% of the global market for electric-vehicle batteries—launched an AI system trained on more than 100,000 battery design cases and 600 terabytes of test data. It cut the time needed to screen candidate materials by two orders of magnitude, according to the company. Independent researchers also found that the ride-sharing platform Didi Chuxing was able cut per-trip emissions 16% on average by using AI-optimized routing to minimize detours.

There’s another big problem in the climate case for AI, though: Oil and gas companies are also interested in these tools for many reasons, including the potential to increase production of fossil fuels. Technology firms that committed to sustainability goals in the past should answer questions about how their tools could be used to boost old, dirty energy as well as clean, renewable energy.

Finally, it’s also worth noting that integrating AI is just the first step, and it remains unclear to what degree the technology can accelerate the sorts of improvements and innovations envisioned. AI also does little to shorten the standard paths to certifying, permitting, building, packaging, marketing, and selling any resulting products, facilities, or services.

The findings of the analysis, carried out by Anthropic’s Claude Code, were reviewed against public documents and media accounts to ensure their accuracy. It is possible that some companies and utilities in these industries are pursuing AI-assisted applications that would fit into the initial study’s scenarios but haven’t yet been disclosed publicly.

Greater and better-targeted AI integration and efficiency gains in these three sectors may well materialize, bringing the 2035 projections closer to reality. But it won’t happen without deliberate and sustained efforts to ensure that AI delivers more environmental benefits than harms.

Notably, so far the rapid adoption of AI models and buildout of data centers has driven a huge increase in energy consumption and emissions, and this has led major tech companies like Microsoft, Google, and others to fall well off track in their corporate climate goals. At this point it would be surprising if the sector even manages to zero out its climate pollution: Emissions associated with global data centers (which may handle AI as well as non-AI computing tasks) could exceed 300 million tons annually in 2035, according to the IEA’s base forecast.

Industry leaders must implement the technology in the ways that promise to do the most to drive down emissions. Corporate customers should select models with the lowest environmental impacts and opt to work with the AI developers doing the most to reduce their climate footprint. And states and regions need to do more to incentivize or require all these companies to operate in more responsible ways.

Mary Nichols is distinguished counsel at the UCLA School of Law and the former chair of the California Air Resources Board.

Jeremy Tamanini is the founder of Dual Citizen, a sustainability research and advisory firm.

These scientists hope that fire-spotting satellites, AI tools and ancient land practices could preserve the world’s vulnerable permafrost—and the vast carbon it stores.

New technology could help cut power losses and reinvent a staple of the grid.

The newly public company is betting that AI’s power boom will help turn enhanced geothermal energy into a scalable reality.

Innovating with AI in the special chemicals industry

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Connected data, AI, and advanced modeling are helping scientists accelerate research and develop more sustainable materials.

As companies look to accelerate innovation with artificial intelligence, cloud computing, and advanced modeling, the primary challenge is connecting new technologies to reliable data, scientific expertise, and the practical realities of bringing products to market. For specialty chemicals manufacturer Lubrizol, digital transformation is all about giving researchers the tools to test ideas faster, make better-informed decisions, and develop more sustainable materials without compromising performance.

Girish Rao, vice president of information technology at Lubrizol, says the company is building a digital foundation that connects business processes, modern data platforms, and cloud infrastructure to support its research and development (R&D) teams. Their goal is to combine decades of accumulated technical data with the deep expertise of chemists and engineers who understand customer needs as well as the physical constraints of their work. Data scientists can help identify patterns, test new hypotheses, and model possibilities before researchers conduct physical experiments. But, Rao emphasizes, “it's not about replacing scientific judgment; it is about giving our scientists better tools.”

Scientists, data scientists, and IT teams approach problems from different perspectives, prioritizing experimental validity, model performance, and platform security and scalability, respectively. By bringing these groups together in “fusion teams,” Lubrizol aligns them around shared objectives, starting with small pilots to build trust.

"Once the trust is built between the team members and they know what value they're about to create," Rao says, "things go a lot better." Cloud infrastructure also gives the company flexibility to access computing resources and modern tools without making the substantial investments required to build and maintain its own data centers.

These capabilities could prove especially valuable in advancing sustainability. Digital modeling can help researchers evaluate more formulations, explore materials such as starch and corn-based alternatives, and assess trade-offs across a product's life cycle. Promising a material in a laboratory is only the first step. Scaling production while meeting performance expectations, regulatory requirements, and financial constraints is still a tightrope walk, but AI's ability to model multiple variables could help teams navigate those competing demands.

Looking to the future, Rao expects AI, automation, advanced modeling, digital twins, and connected data to converge as laboratories become more intelligent and manufacturing plants more efficient. The opportunity, he suggests, lies in bringing these capabilities together responsibly and at scale. “It's not just a single technology. I think it's the combination of all of these, and it'll be the ability to connect all these processes that are going to generate tremendous amounts of data."

King Arthur Baking Sourdough Sidekick Review: Rising to the Occasion

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Like many people, I enjoy baking my own bread—especially this time of year. I baked sourdough on and off for nearly a decade, even having a recipe tested for a James Beard-winning bread cookbook, but I’m not proud to admit it’s been over a year since I baked a proper sourdough loaf.

As a full-time working parent, maintaining a sourdough starter sometimes feels like bringing home another pet. (You know, the kind your child swears they will care for before abandoning all tasks after three days.) You can certainly keep your starter in the fridge for around a week between bakes, but reviving it may take a day or two of feedings, and let’s be real, Will you remember to do this every week? Even the pet snake my son begged for only needs feeding every other week.

It is for people like me that King Arthur Baking Company and FirstBuild, GE Appliances’ product development lab, created the Sourdough Sidekick, a gizmo that automatically feeds and maintains a sourdough starter for you. We wrote about the Sourdough Sidekick when it was first announced in 2025, and now, after trying it for three weeks, I can say that my sourdough-baking dry spell has finally come to an end, perhaps even permanently.

Sourdough Sidekick, pictured here with the optional Crock Grip Band ($15)

The Sidekick is a Mr. Coffee-sized appliance with a built-in water reservoir, flour hopper, and glass crock with a stirring-paddle lid. I deal with an awful lot of apps in my job, and the Sidekick uses GE’s generic SmartHQ for GE appliances, which isn’t specific to this device and seemed optional. I admit I couldn’t be bothered with an unnecessary app, so I didn’t download it.

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

Instead, I followed the directions in the included Quick Start Guide booklet, which indicated that I simply needed to fill the water reservoir and flour hopper, set the current day and time (and the day and time I wanted the starter to be ready—you can choose up to seven days ahead; I chose seven), add a little bit of existing starter, and press “Auto Mode.” Almost immediately, a torrent of flour released from the hopper and began mixing into my seed starter with the stirring paddle. (King Arthur recommends keeping a portion of your starter in the fridge in case things go wrong, and I second this suggestion.)

Oddly, no water was dispensed in this process, so post-mixing, my starter was now in the form of an under-hydrated ball. Over the next few days, it became a larger, drier ball until I finally decided to remove the crock to add a couple of tablespoons of water; as I did, I noticed the reservoir was still at its maximum fill line.

I decided I should probably download the app, where I noticed the Sidekick indicated it needed a firmware update. After the update, the Sidekick began dispensing water during mixing, but I realized I really should have followed the instructions in the main instruction booklet, which calls for using a scale to properly calibrate the amounts of water and flour the machine dispenses.

Theoretically, you should only have to do this once, which is good because it’s an irritatingly lengthy and fiddly process. However, my next attempt (remember when I mentioned saving some starter in the fridge?) went much better, properly dispensing 30 grams each of water and flour each day and initiating a stirring cycle every two hours. It starts with a loud beep, then the paddle begins a loud mechanical whir, and for the first few days this can be quite startling, especially if you’re in the same room when it happens.

You can bypass the every-two-hours stirring cycle via the onboard controls, but since the stirring’s purpose is to knock down the starter’s volume and reincorporate any unconsumed flour, I kept it on. (For what it’s worth, I did think the paddle, an update from the Sidekick’s first design, did a pretty good job of incorporating all of the flour and water each time it mixed.)

Note that, in part due to the regular mixing and the lack of discard (Auto Mode will prompt you to discard after a week, while Ratio and Custom modes, detailed below, will have discard reminders), your finished starter in the Sidekick may not look as traditionally puffy or bubbly as you may be used to. You can disable stirring, or you can go on scent—it should smell tangy and indisputably fermented. As the many online Sidekick adherents often say, “Trust the process.” Some online users recommended adding a few teaspoons of more nutritionally dense rye flour to the (presumably all-purpose) flour in the hopper, which is supposed to supercharge the starter’s growth, but I admit I didn’t notice much of a difference in appearance, smell, or performance between an all-purpose starter and a rye-boosted starter.

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

Boules made with end-of-week starter maintained via the Sourdough Sidekick

Other than enabling firmware updates, the app does little beyond displaying the machine’s current settings and sending a push notification when the starter is finished. You can’t adjust settings or control the machine from it, but the most recent firmware update supports power-loss recovery up to 12 hours, which I tested and found successful after my husband tripped a breaker one day. The display shows the ambient temperature, but unlike other starter-babysitting gadgets like the Goldie or Brod & Taylor’s Sourdough Home, the Sourdough Sidekick has no way to adjust the starter’s temperature, which could be an issue if your house or kitchen runs cold. It does, however, offer “temperature-aware” feedings in Auto Mode, adjusting amounts based on detected room temperature.

In addition to Auto Mode, there’s Ratio Mode (where you can indicate starter-flour-water feedings by ratio—1:1:1, 1:1:2, et al.) and Custom Mode, which is what I ended up using the most, as I like to switch between flour types for different kinds of bread—you can dictate the exact flour and water quantities, as well as timing of the feeding (once a day, or even twice a day). In a typical week, the crock was about two-thirds full by the end, with more than 400 grams of starter—enough for two boules and a sandwich loaf with some seed left over for the next batch—ready to use right out of the crock.

The resulting bread turned out no differently than it had with traditionally maintained starter. If you’re unable to bake at that time, you can just discard most of the starter, leaving some “seed” behind for the next batch, or keep it in the refrigerator for a few days before reviving it the traditional way. (The crock comes with a metal lid for this purpose.)

King Arthur Baking the Sourdough Sidekick

If you’re only baking one loaf, you’ll have excess, but you can use the discard in recipes or give it away. Just note that if you intend to make a starter with a different flour, like whole wheat or full rye, you will need to calibrate the machine again as you did at the beginning. All removable components are top-rack dishwasher safe.

The Sourdough Sidekick is one of the most compelling kitchen-tech gifts I’ve ever seen for someone who bakes a lot or is interested in sourdough baking but intimidated by the process of keeping a starter. (The Sidekick also includes directions for making your own starter right in the machine.) Is it a necessary device? Of course not—especially since it costs about $175 more than a spoon and a Mason jar. But I can tell you I wouldn’t have baked bread this year without it.

The Download: empty AI climate claims, and an Nvidia investigation

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Plus: Democrats are planning a blitz of investigations into Big Tech.

This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology.

—By Mary Nichols, distinguished counsel at the UCLA School of Law and the former chair of the California Air Resources Board, and Jeremy Tamanini, the founder of Dual Citizen, a sustainability research and advisory firm

This week, as countries meet in Antalya, Turkey for the United Nations climate conference, one topic is likely to dominate the conversation: artificial intelligence. The host has already called on countries to commit to ensuring that AI is designed, powered, and deployed in ways that support climate action.

But so far, industries around the world aren’t doing nearly enough to harness the technology to actually drive down the greenhouse-gas emissions heating up the planet. AI companies like to make very optimistic claims about AI’s potential to help address climate change. The reality is far less promising. Read the full story.

Tropical forests are our planet’s biggest terrestrial carbon sink, accounting for around 40% of carbon stored on land. Forest ecologist Daisy Dent, a senior scientist at ETH Zurich, is part of an international network of researchers working to decipher the networks of animal and plant interactions that enable some forests to recover after destruction.

The goal is a low-cost, high-impact alternative to typical carbon-­crediting schemes, which plant huge areas with a single species of tree. For over a decade, her research has helped show that these large “monocrop” programs could be expensive mistakes—and that more resilient forests can rebound naturally in just a few decades, re-creating ecosystems that suck up vast quantities of carbon dioxide.

Learn more about Dent, her work, and her impact on how we understand the role rainforests play in regulating our climate.

EU aims to pick new ECB chief by year-end

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Christine Lagarde's decision to step down early as head of the European Central Bank (ECB) has put the European Union on a tight schedule. The bloc expects to pick a new ECB chief by the end of December.

The European Union (EU) wants to name the next head of the European Central Bank by the end of the year, Eurogroup chief Kyriakos Pierrakakis said Monday.

Christine Lagarde, who has headed the ECB for seven years, announced on October 29 that she is stepping down from the position prematurely, nine months ⁠before ⁠the end of her term.

Lagarde's decision to step down early makes the schedule tight for EU leaders because they must also fill two other seats on the ECB's Executive Board ​within months.

"The ambition here is that the whole process would have completed itself by the end of the year," Pierrakakis said before a Eurogroup meeting of eurozone finance ministers.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"We understand that we need to move fast. But what has traditionally happened in ​the past is that this was a decision driven by the leaders. Today it will be primarily an information point and the discussion," he said.

The two candidates to replace Lagarde are former Dutch Central Bank Governor Klaas Knot and the head of the Bank for International ​Settlements, Pablo ‌Hernandez de Cos from Spain.

Pierrakakis said that Dutch and Spanish ministers would present their candidates and that later on Monday he would meet with Antonio Costa, head of the European Council, which represents EU governments' heads.

The appointment process requires eurozone finance ministers to put forward a candidate, followed by a European Parliament hearing and a final decision by EU leaders.

Spanish Economy Minister Carlos Cuerpo said Hernandez de Cos was the "right person for the position," referring to his long-standing experience.

"He knows the ECB inside out. He's been leading institutions such as the Bank of Spain, such as BIS as well. A steady hand, but always a profile to be able to find consensus," he told reporters.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Dutch Finance Minister Eelco Heinen, on the other hand, said Knot was an "excellent candidate", adding: "He has a very clear understanding of the ECB mandate and he has... a lot of experience to fulfill this position."

Heinen also ​said countries should ‌not see the ECB leadership contest as ​one between northern ⁠and southern European countries, or as ⁠an "​opportunity to trade favors" on other topics such as the multi-year EU budget.

The other two seats which have to be filled are those of Isabel ‌Schnabel, the head of the ECB's market operations, who leaves on January 3, 2027, and Chief Economist Philip Lane, ​whose term ends ⁠in May 2027.

The three jobs are likely to be negotiated between the ⁠main eurozone countries — ​France, Germany, Spain and the Netherlands — as a package, Reuters news agency said, citing officials.

The expectation is that the leaders agree on a package deal on all three positions.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Perhaps this would facilitate things. But again, this is a decision that will be taken by the leaders at the leaders level," Pierrakakis said.

Irish Finance Minister Simon Harris, whose country holds the rotating EU presidency, said he hoped Lagarde's replacement would be chosen by December.

"It certainly makes sense to fill the vacancy as quickly as possible," he said. But it's also about getting it right and getting the right person. It's a really crucial appointment."

Denzel Washington's Déjà Vu Is Now Streaming For Free On Tubi

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Denzel Washington is a master in practically every genre. Whether he's taking out bad guys in action movies like The Equalizer, delivering Shakespeare in dramas like The Tragedy of Macbeth, or playing devilishly charming antagonists in films like Gladiator II, he can do it convincingly.

That has allowed the two-time Oscar winner to prove time and again the breadth of his abilities, with thrillers becoming a genre that he goes back to all the time. He's done some incredible action thrillers, like Crimson Tide and Man on Fire, as well as those of the crime variety with Training Day and Inside Man; it's a realm he's poised to return to with his upcoming movie with Christopher McQuarrie.

But a genre Washington hasn't explored as much is sci-fi. He took a big swing with Virtuosity opposite Russell Crowe in 1995, while he later went post-apocalyptic to make The Book of Eli in 2010. Between those two films, Washington made Déjà Vu, a sci-fi thriller that is currently free to stream.

Déjà Vu is a 2006 sci-fi action thriller by director Tony Scott, in which Washington plays the lead role of ATF agent Doug Carlin. The movie follows his character as he investigates the fallout of a domestic terrorist attack, only to learn of a top-secret time-traveling device he can use to possibly prevent it from ever happening.

Running 2 hours and 6 minutes in length, Déjà Vu is now streaming for free on Tubi. The ad-supported service makes the movie accessible to anyone without requiring a streaming subscription. The film came to Tubi on November 1, 2026, and will stay on the platform for the entire month.

Washington is certainly the headliner of Déjà Vu's cast, but it has a stacked ensemble around him. Paula Patton is the victim he's trying to save, while Jim Caviezel is the wanted criminal. Val Kilmer and Bruce Greenwood join the investigation as FBI agents, while Adam Goldberg, Matt Craven, Elden Henson, and Erika Alexander are among the other cast members.

Déjà Vu was the third of five Denzel Washington and Tony Scott movies, but it is easily the most underappreciated. Crimson Tide and Unstoppable are highly regarded, while Man on Fire is frequently reclaimed as an excellent action thriller. Déjà Vu deserves the same treatment after critics were initially mixed on the sci-fi thriller, which is why it has a 55% on Rotten Tomatoes.

However, Denzel Washington and Tony Scott's collaborations are always entertaining, and Déjà Vu is no different. It's a thrilling sci-fi movie with a strong ensemble cast, solid directing, strong visuals, and more. Denzel's underrated movie works a lot better than its Rotten Tomatoes score suggests, explaining why audiences responded so well that it made $180 million 20 years ago.

Unsurprisingly, Denzel's lead performance is a big reason why. He is as electric as expected, making Doug's desperation to save the day, pining after Patton's character, and intellect come across without sacrificing his charisma. He has a great back-and-forth with Kilmer, while his standoff with Caviezel is captivating.

Scott's direction keeps the film moving and packed with good action set pieces. The film easily could've become confusing due to how it explores time, but the director navigates the past and present seamlessly. And while the time-travel logic was a point of confusion during Déjà Vu's release, the rules established are not that different from time travel movies that came after and were well-received.

Now that Déjà Vu is on streaming, its a great choice for anyone to watch who is a fan of Denzel, Tony Scott, or sci-fi thrillers. Whether you want to watch it again or for the first time, just don't wait too long and miss the film before it is no longer streaming for free on Tubi.

37 years after fall of Berlin Wall: How divided are Germans?

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Three artists share their thoughts on the reunification of East and West Germany. How do they perceive a shared "we" and what gives them cause for optimism?

When the Berlin Wall fell on November 9, 1989, ending East Germany's isolation from the West, the event was celebrated on both sides of the border. Yet, 37 years later, many people on both sides remain deeply skeptical of each other.

Surveys show that only a minority of respondents report feeling a strong sense of community between those from former East and West Germany, and many continue to perceive significant differences.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

We asked three artists from Germany with different perspectives on the history of the country's division to share their views.

Author Lea Streisand was born in 1979 and experienced the fall of the Berlin Wall firsthand while growing up in East Berlin.

In her books and essays, she has returned to her East German upbringing many times, using literature to process it.

The novel "Hufeland, at the corner of Bötzow" (2019, Ullstein), for example, recounts the period of reunification from a child's perspective.

"November 9, 1989, was simply incredible," she told DW. She was 10 years old at the time. When her mother bought her winter boots with the 100 Deutsche Marks she received from the West German state — a welcome gift given to all East German citizens — she was so disappointed that she felt like crying. But later, a friend of her parents' let her buy so many sweets at the supermarket that they lasted until Christmas.

"The disappointment really set in afterwards, with what happened over the course of the following year. It was unbelievable how quickly an entire system was suddenly swept away and a new one built up, as if to say, 'There you go. You're welcome'," Streisand recalled. "I think it was just too fast for people."

The people of East Germany, or the GDR, had brought about the fall of the Berlin Wall themselves through a civil rights movement and numerous demonstrations. For many, however, the goal was not to dissolve the state immediately but to reform it.

Among them was Anke Feuchtenberger, born in 1963 in East Berlin. Her childhood was spent in East Berlin and in the East German region of Western Pomerania, where she now lives again and feels "very much at home."

Today, she teaches at the University of Applied Sciences in Hamburg, meaning she spends time on both sides of the former divide. Feuchtenberger is often described as Germany's most influential comic artist. Her work has also garnered international acclaim.

"Back then, as artists, we tried to bring about a change through posters and flyers — to gain more freedom without immediately abolishing socialism," she explained.

At the time, the fall of the Berlin Wall stirred up mixed feelings in Feuchtenberger: "I soon realized that the creative and revolutionary aspects of our movement were very quickly being smothered by consumerism, and that nationalist tendencies — which had already been noticeable before — were coming to the fore," Feuchtenberger recalled. "That made me afraid."

Meanwhile, for photographic artist Simon Menner, born in 1978 in Emmendingen, in the West German state of Baden-Württemberg, Berlin was very far away. As Menner put it, he experienced the fall of the Berlin Wall as a child in a "rather abstract" way. Today, Berlin is his home.

In his experience, the way films and documentaries portrayed East Germany around the time of reunification was very bleak. "But West Germany in the '80s," he said, "was also bleak and very staid."

This uneven portrayal of the two parts of Germany is an issue that deeply concerns Menner — partly because he unwittingly contributed to it in a small way. Among other things, Menner's work explores the topic of surveillance and how it works.

In the archives of East Germany's Ministry for State Security, or Stasi — which are freely accessible today as part of the National Archives — he discovered a massive collection of photographs that both fascinated and horrified him and which he wanted to bring to the attention of a wider public.

His photo book "Top Secret" (2012, from publisher Hatje Cantz) features a selection of photos that offer a unique insight into the work of the Stasi. Some are eerie, while others are curious or even absurd.

Menner also tried to look into the photo archive of West Germany's Federal Intelligence Service. "I spent a year trying to gain access — without success," he said.

In hindsight, he now finds his artistic project problematic because he believes it is too one-sided, focusing exclusively on East Germany's past.

According to Menner, West Germany's intelligence services in the 1960s were not exactly models of virtue either, and he believes it is "actually high time" to critically examine this part of history as well. In his view, an honest examination of Germany's past as a whole is a prerequisite for genuine reconciliation.

Streisand believes literature can help foster mutual understanding. "Literature opens a window into experiences we haven't had ourselves," she said. "You can immerse yourself in the mind of someone who has had a different life than you have and understand what happened."

Interest in these stories remains strong. In 2023, Feuchtenberger published the nearly 500-page graphic novel "Comrade Cuckoo" (published by Reprodukt), which was the first graphic novel to be nominated for the Leipzig Book Fair Prize in the fiction category.

The story spans a childhood in the fictional East German village of Pritschitanow in the 1960s in the GDR to the post-Reunification era. In it, Feuchtenberger weaves autobiographical memories of her upbringing with fantastical and fictional elements.

The artist said she was surprised "that, so long after the fall of the wall, my stories have sparked such great interest in the West."

In particular, the fact that the main character, Kerstin, rarely sees her parents because they both work full-time is a topic that often comes up at readings. In the GDR, everyone had the right to work, she said. At the same time, it was a civic duty.

Could the West German interest in this stem from the fact that full-time employment for both parents has long been less common in the West? It's possible, said Feuchtenberger.

When looking toward their shared future, the two East German women are more optimistic than Menner, from West Germany.

"In Berlin, I often don't even notice anymore whether I'm in the West or the East," said Menner. "But when it comes to sociopolitical issues, politicians should listen to older and younger people more closely," he added, referring to the recent election successes of far-right Alternative for Germany, or AfD . "Otherwise, things are going to get difficult in the near future."

"It's very important to step outside our own bubble because people all over the world are facing problems. Constantly focusing on this German-German divide that has emerged between the groups doesn't help," said Feuchtenberger.

"For me personally, the whole issue of borders has somehow dissolved," she explained. This is due in no small part to the many trips abroad that have taken her to various places around the world.

Moreover, in her hometown near the Polish border, she observes how animals couldn't care less about the border. "They come from the east and the west and do whatever they want," she said. That's why she also gave "Comrade Cuckoo" the working title "A German Animal in a German Forest," "because I found the absurdity of an animal having a nationality so funny."

"This East-West thing — our children won't have any interest in it at all," said Streisand. "Young people don't care where their parents come from. Can I get a job that will support a family? Do I have to go to war? Right now, completely different things are relevant."

This article was originally written in German.

Russell Brand sentenced to over 10 years in prison for rape and sexual assault convictions

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

The former comedian was found guilty of raping one woman and sexually assaulting three others last week. The judge referred to Brand as a "predator."

British media personality Russell Brand was sentenced to 10 years and 6 months imprisonment on Monday after being found guilty of rape and assault.

He is required to spend at least two-thirds of that time in prison, and may be released under supervision after that if he displays good conduct while in jail.

Judge Joel Bennathan called Brand a "predator," and said that the sentence is "just and proportionate...to the overall gravity of your criminality."

Bennathan also praised the "determination and bravery" of the survivors who came forward.

The 51-year-old had consistently denied the charges, brought about by six victims on allegations going back as far as 1999.

A jury of four women and eight men did not find his defense that all the encounters were consensual to be plausible, and found him guilty of raping or sexually assaulting four of his accusers.

At the sentencing hearing, some of his victims gave victim impact statements to the court.

"I wanted ‌to express how this crime has affected me over the years," one woman said from behind a screen to protect her privacy, adding that the attack led to an "undeserved feeling of shame" she had carried for 20 years.

Another survivor called Brand a "sexual predator who has no remorse."

UK Prime Minister Andy Burnham hailed the bravery of the survivors in a statement, and said his government would be "relentless in making whatever improvements we can” to provide more support for victims and make sure every police station has a sexual assault response team.

Brand rose to prominence in the UK stand-up scene in the early 2000s with sets focused on his sex life, alcoholism, and heroin addiction.

He then became a presenter for several British television shows before starring in several Hollywood movies and being in a brief, high-profile marriage to American pop star Katy Perry.

As accusations of predatory behavior toward women began to mount, Brand left London and Hollywood behind for Florida. His politics changed from left-wing activism to promoting conservative ideas and conspiracy theories on his podcast.

He has said he underwent a religious conversion around this time. In 2016 he resumed his relationship with restauranteur Laura Gallacher, whom he first dated in 2007 when he was 31 and she was 19. They have since had three children together. Gallacher has remained by Brand's side throughout the court proceedings.

Strait of Hormuz still a source of leverage for Iran

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Attacks in the Strait of Hormuz put crews at risk and drive up energy shipping costs. Iran's ability to sustain them and the limits of US protection will shape how much trade can continue and at what price.

Since the Iran war started, attacks on commercial ships in the Strait of Hormuz have put crews at risk of death and injury, forcing shipping companies to choose whether to attempt the passage or delay cargos and face higher costs.

Merchant vessels continue to use the strait despite the danger, but how long that can continue depends partly on Iran's ability to sustain its attacks and the United States' capacity to protect shipping and bear the cost.

Ali Mohammadi, an expert in Iranian affairs based in France, believes Iran's geography and the equipment it uses allow it to keep attacking ships.

But he sees the economic burden of the war as Tehran's main constraint. "Iran's limitation in the Strait of Hormuz is economic rather than military, because continuing the war has a significant impact on its economy," he said.

It's unclear how long Iran can sustain attacks given the lack of reliable information on the nation's weapons stocks and its ability to replenish supplies. And sporadic attacks in the crucial waterway are also different from closing the strait completely. "Even if a complete closure of the Strait of Hormuz were possible, it would not be sustainable because it could carry high political costs for Iran," Mohammadi told DW. "Iran cannot indefinitely disrupt its allies' access to the strait." By threatening shipping, Iran can raise the cost of confrontation and seek leverage in negotiations but that may not guarantee success or yield concessions, the expert said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Data from the UK Maritime Trade Operations Center's (UKMTO) weekly report, released on November 6, show 325 reported transits through Hormuz between October 29 and November 4. That was up from 277 the previous week, an increase of about 17%.

It's still roughly 65% below pre-war levels, the UKMTO assessed.

But vessel numbers alone cannot explain what is happening to energy supplies as cargo volumes and the routes used also matter, said Dalga Khatinoglu, an expert on Iran's energy and macroeconomics.

"If we look only at crude oil, Middle Eastern exports across all routes, from the Gulf of Oman and the Strait of Hormuz to the Red Sea, have largely recovered to pre-war levels," he told DW. "In some weeks in September and October, they even exceeded those levels."

His assessment covers the region's overall crude exports across all routes, not just shipments passing through the Strait of Hormuz.

The picture for refined petroleum products is different, Khatinoglu said. "Before the war, the Middle East exported about 6.2 million barrels of petroleum products a day. That figure has now roughly halved." Liquefied natural gas (LNG) exports have also suffered. "As of September, exports from Qatar and the UAE were about 10% below pre-war levels. Although there has been some improvement, they remain severely disrupted," the expert underscored.

Khatinoglu said recovering energy exports have not eliminated the costs associated with ongoing insecurity.

"Although oil exports from Middle Eastern countries have approached pre-war levels, shipping and insurance costs have remained very high," he stressed.

Citing recent figures from the Organization of the Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC), he said that average freight rates for a large tanker carrying crude from the Middle East to East Asia has risen about 12 times, from $3.18 (€2,84)  per barrel at the beginning of the year to $37.13 a barrel at the end of October.

This means elevated costs for Asian importers even when crude is available.

Reducing costs and restoring commercial confidence in the safety of the strait requires more than a US military presence, Mohammadi said.

"There is only one solution to the Strait of Hormuz issue and that's an end to the war, accompanied by a lasting agreement that brings lasting security," he said.

Khatinoglu shared a similar view. "You cannot expect oil prices to return to pre-war levels simply by relying on US protection for ships in the Strait of Hormuz," he said.

Nine foreigners caught allegedly trying to leave South Africa to fight in Ukraine

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of African citizens have been recruited to join the Russia-Ukraine war in recent years (file photo)

Nine foreigners have been arrested in South Africa after they were caught allegedly trying to leave the country to fight in the war between Russia and the Ukraine, police say.

The group, aged between 20 and 26, were arrested in and around Johannesburg by a multidisciplinary team on Sunday evening.

They were caught as they were "preparing to board flights out of the country", authorities added, but their final destination is unclear.

The group is accused of contravening South Africa's laws, which outlaw mercenary activity without government permission.

Police said the group were arrested at South Africa's biggest airport, OR Tambo, and a hotel in Bruma, east of Johannesburg by a multidisciplinary team that involved Interpol.

The police statement added that the involvement of Interpol and other international law enforcement agencies indicated that the suspects had also allegedly violated laws in their respective home countries, prompting cooperation with South African authorities.

Authorities declined to disclose the group's nationalities but confirmed that none of them resided in South Africa.

They have also not said whether the group allegedly intended to fight for Russia or Ukraine nor provided details of the organisations allegedly involved in facilitating their travel.

Citizens of several African countries, including Kenya, Ghana and South Africa, have gone to fight for Russia in Ukraine - some they had been duped and had not realised where they were going.

A Ukrainian intelligence assessment estimated earlier this year that more than 1,700 people from 36 countries in Africa have been recruited to fight for Russia.

Kenya at the time estimated that 252 citizens had been illegally conscripted in Russia. In March, Foreign Minister Musalia Mudavadi visited Moscow to address the issue.

He said at the time that an agreement had been reached to stop recruiting Kenyan nationals.

In February, Ghana said that 55 of its citizens had been killed in the conflict, with 272 believed to have been lured to fight since the war started in 2022.

The same month, South Africa secured the return of 17 South Africans who were allegedly tricked into joining the war.

In December last year, South African authorities arrested five people, including a journalist, for allegedly recruiting people to join the war.

Ukraine has also been recruiting foreign nationals, including from Africa.

The nine arrested are expected to appear in court either later on Monday or Tuesday.

Trump says he was 'kidding' about Dana White press secretary offer

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Trump initially asked the UFC boss to be press secretary on Saturday

US President Donald Trump has said he "was kidding" about asking Ultimate Fighting Championship (UFC) boss Dana White to be the White House press secretary.

Trump told the media on Sunday that White "loves the idea" but that the invitation was "just a little fun".

It comes after the president posted on his Truth Social platform on Saturday that he had offered White the role, asking him to take it while writing, "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other."

White said later that day he was going to meet the president on Monday, but would not be drawn into whether or not he would take the vacant position.

On Sunday Trump told reporters White was visiting him on Monday for "other reasons".

"I think he's going to be in Washington. So he's going to come over and see me.

"I will tell you, I was only kidding. But Dana loves the idea. And there's only one Dana. He's great," the president said.

The press secretary role is yet to be filled after it became vacant in October when Katie Zacharia quit after less than five days in the job.

She took over from Karoline Leavitt, who was the youngest press secretary in US history and had held the role since Trump began his second term.

Trump's job offer came after a heavy loss in the midterm elections, where the Republican Party lost its majority in both houses of Congress.

In his Truth Social post on Saturday, he said he was "pleased to announce" that White "has been offered the job".

He said he had asked White to bring with him several current and former mixed martial arts fighters as "assistants".

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," he wrote.

Asked about the Trump offer at a news conference on Saturday, the UFC chief executive said he had "lot of jobs already".

"I'm sure we'll have something to talk about after that," he said of their planned Monday meeting, but did not say whether or not he would take the job.

White leads UFC, and PowerSlap, a regulated slap-fighting promotion league, as well as recently launching promotion company Zuffa Boxing.

He is also a board member of Meta, the owner of Facebook, Instagram and WhatsApp.

White appeared as a speaker in support of Trump's White House campaigns at the Republican National Conventions in 2016, 2020 and 2024.

He also appeared on stage for Trump's victory speech in the 2024 presidential election.

This summer, the UFC staged a cage match on the White House lawn.

Inside the French drug gangs recruiting children to kill

📅 2026-11-09  |  Read Source →

Watch: "It destroys people, but we all make our own choices" - Marseille drug-gang members talk to BBC

Warning: Strong language and descriptions of sexual violence

Although she had just bought hashish from a drug dealer, the woman decided to call the police.

It was because, along with the illegal drugs, she had been handed a note by the young seller during their brief meeting on a Marseille housing estate in 2022.

"We're being held captive and need help. We are 15-years-old," it read.

The teenage dealer, a French court heard earlier this year, was one of two boys being held by a gang which they had both initially approached looking for paid work - albeit illegal.

One boy had been tasked with handling money, the other worked streets around the Frais-Vallon estate. Soon though, bosses had accused them of being responsible for "shortfalls" in takings.

What followed were regular beatings, the court was told, and being forced to dance naked in front of a camera as a form of blackmail. One of the boys was also raped.

Hours after the woman was passed the note, the two teenagers had tried to escape from a second-storey window, injuring themselves in the process. One managed to get away, while the other was caught, beaten and stabbed, but survived.

Arrests were made. Photos on the phone of one of the boys' alleged torturers showed several other young men in a similarly distressed condition. Six men have since been handed hefty sentences for what happened to the two teenagers.

The boys' story highlights the grip that drug gangs hold over parts of France's second city.

While the blend of Mediterranean culture, street art and hip-hop scene has made Marseille a popular tourist destination - beyond the city centre, it is another story.

After months of trying, a BBC team managed to enter this dangerous world - centred on deprived streets and high-rise housing estates in the port city, which police do not enter lightly.

We found an environment where ruthless gangs lure vulnerable young people with promises of easy money, but end up trapping them in a world of drug dealing, brutality and exploitation.

We saw how gangs use social media to recruit hitmen and spoke to several young men caught up in the violence. From prison, one gang member accused of involvement in several murders explained how his "cold-bloodedness" had helped him rise through the ranks.

A still image from one of DZ Mafia's notorious video statements

The city's most powerful gang, DZ Mafia, emerged victorious from a bloody turf war and is now one of the most feared criminal organisations in France.

DZ is the official internet country code for Algeria, where one of the gang's founders, a French-Algerian called Mehdi Laribi, was detained earlier this year under a French arrest warrant.

The criminal outfit rose to prominence in 2023, when it became the first French gang to capitalise on its notoriety by releasing video statements - using social media to market itself as a brand - says the head of the country's anti-drug trafficking unit, Dimitri Zoulas.

Its most recent statement came in July, when the mayor of a nearby town had to be placed in police protection, after intelligence suggested he was in danger of being targeted by the group.

In an online video, six masked men dressed in black stood behind a table - as one of them read a statement denying DZ Mafia's involvement.

France's expanding illegal drugs market - which has seen a surge in cocaine imports alongside high volumes of cannabis and synthetic drugs - has also helped the gang grow rapidly.

The number of cocaine users in France is expected soon to reach two million, says Zoulas, up from 600,000 in 2017. Drug smuggling across France generates â¬7bn (Â£5.9bn) a year, involving 200,000 people, he adds.

Many of those distributing the drugs are children and the authorities say the number of minors caught up in trafficking is increasing. During 2025, more than 10,000 children were involved in some way, according to official figures.

DZ Mafia is a prime example. To reach more customers, it needs more gang members - more children.

We have found it is now recruiting young people from across France - not just to sell drugs, but to carry out kidnap, torture and murder.

They are a generation seen as easy to corrupt, but also expendable, by the kingpins who typically control them from prison or overseas.

And, as police and judges warn, once these children enter Marseille's underworld, it is very hard to get them out.

Karine was called by a doctor about her son Hugo: "Your son arrived last night. He took a bullet to the head"

Few know the human cost of Marseille's gangs like mother Karine Brouillard - who lives at the other end of France, almost as far from the Mediterranean coast as you can get.

Her son, Hugo, first ran away aged 15 - from his home in a village in the Nord department - after an argument with his family. He was picked up by police three days later, almost 1,000km (600 miles) away in Marseille, with money and cannabis on him. He was taken home.

But several years later, in 2023, Hugo planned to return south, telling his parents he had secured a holiday job at a restaurant near Marseille.

"He gave regular updates," Karine remembers. "He called me, everything was going well."

Three weeks later, a doctor called. Karine says she will never forget his words: "Your son arrived last night. He took a bullet to the head."

Police believe Hugo was brought for medical help from a gang-controlled area and suspect he was involved with one.

DZ Mafia wields power in Marseille's Quartier Nord

Our best way into Quartier Nord, where DZ Mafia wields power, is with the help of resident Fadella Ouidef, a 41-year-old mother-of-four who gives us a tour.

Thirty-seven seconds after entering one housing estate, we find ourselves cornered.

Car horns sound the moment Fadella's Peugeot passes a gang checkpoint - the teenagers manning the post immediately spot our camera. "Wait, stop filming," Fadella warns, as a grey 4x4 forces us to the side of the road.

"I'm making a report for my housing association," she tells the inquisitive driver. He eventually agrees to let us leave if we stop filming.

It is unnerving to see a non-police vehicle move so assertively and a relief to watch it leave.

"Lately, the gangs have been very aggressive," explains Fadella. "What we simply try to do is make sure our young people understand what's happening is wrong."

Fadella speaks to a wider fear among local families: that their children are enticed by huge sums of money to take a criminal path that robs them of their innocence.

Camil, who claims to have left DZ Mafia, only agreed to speak to the BBC away from the city - on a boat

Money is what led one young man to spend his teenage years with DZ Mafia.

In order for our interviewees to speak freely, or because of safety concerns, we have agreed to protect their identities.

Camil tells us he worked up the gang's ranks to "running security" in one area it controls.

The now 20-year-old, who claims to have left the group, is highly suspicious of our presence, but agrees to tell us how the gang recruits young members.

"They know how to get inside your head. Whatever you want, they give to you," he says. This can range from nice cars and spacious apartments, to drugs and prostitutes, he explains.

"You feel loyalty because of what they give you. You'll stay until the very end, until either death or prison."

Camil also gives a sobering assessment of what happens if the gang identifies a weakness in a recruit: "They'll exploit it. You're their bitch, it's over."

DZ Mafia member, Adam - not his real name - tells us he was part of a DZ Mafia hit squad when we speak to him in a Snapchat video call.

He is currently in prison awaiting trial, after being implicated in several murders.

"People noticed my determination, my cold-bloodedness and, without wanting to, I climbed up the ladder of the gang," says Adam. "It became my place, I had to roll with it."

During the chat, he agrees the killings he has been charged with were "quite shocking".

"Attempts in broad daylight, in traffic jamsâ¦ it can range from going to hit a guy, slap him to taking him in front of his mother and burning him up in a car. I think I have good composure, I handle it fairly well."

"Thank you, see you soon" reads graffiti on a wall, alongside price lists for cocaine, hashish and marijuana

We challenge Adam on whether he feels regret when carrying out such acts of brutality.

He says he is "feeling in-part guilty" but claims to have no regrets.

"If I hadn't done all of this, there are other people who would have done that in my place, and so on. I am just another brick in the wall," he says.

Like so many teenagers from Marseille, he knew large sums of money could be earned quickly in drug trafficking. Now in his twenties, he admits the environment he entered was a "trap".

At a so-called deal point in a social housing estate - sealed off and controlled by DZ Mafia - price lists for cocaine, hashish and marijuana are graffitied on a wall.

The estate acts as a natural fortress for drug dealers. With one major road in and out, they can control the flow of customers and see if the police are coming.

It is not uncommon for the sound of automatic gunfire to fill the night air in these areas.

After months of negotiations, a deal-point manager from a rival gang agrees to meet us where he works.

Gang member "Joe" says he was making â¬3,000 (Â£2,500) per month aged 13

We enter the estate early in the morning, the birdsong and children walking to school providing a stark contrast to the industrial-scale criminal operation that takes place here.

"Joe" says he was making â¬3,000 (Â£2,500) a month when he was 13. Now he says that has risen to between â¬5,000 and â¬10,000 a month.

He seems to see his life path as pre-ordained, albeit a lucrative one.

We ask Joe if he thinks about the lives lost because of the violence carried out by his gang, but the 23-year-old has also long made peace with the pain and destruction drug trafficking can cause.

"It destroys people, but we all make our own choices," he shrugs.

The work that Joe's gang and DZ Mafia are doing represents a threat to national security, says Zoulas, of the anti-drug trafficking unit.

There are road accidents, as well as family violence, depressions, suicides and more than 700 overdoses each year, he says, "and it distorts the economy".

"We are heading towards a potential systemic breakdown of the criminal justice system."

DZ Mafia members filmed firing guns from a balcony in Marseille - the footage was then posted on social media

Police have had success in Marseille and other cities against drug gangs.

Criminal operations are being frustrated, more kingpins arrested and drugs seized.

Since 2024, about 1,000 individuals associated with DZ Mafia have been arrested, and 600 incarcerated, police say.

But overall, it is a battle that is becoming more arduous, rather than easier.

As gangs seek to widen their operations, or replace members who have been arrested or killed, social media has become a potent recruitment tool - especially since the Covid pandemic, when many street-drug sales jumped online.

On social media, we found countless posts advertising jobs in Marseille for aspiring drivers, couriers and hitmen, often with glamourised AI-generated images.

Nets are also being cast wider, as it becomes harder to entice young people locally because they know more about what might happen to them.

The ads can reach teenagers in most corners of France - including Paris, Nantes and Lille - and serve as a powerful pull to Marseille, and the Quartier Nord, police say.

Online advertising has also changed the typical profile of someone who would join a gang's ranks, say law enforcement officials.

Now, it is often someone without a criminal past, but who has exploitable vulnerabilities, like having a difficult family life.

"These people are recruited by unscrupulous sponsors who send them into the lion's den," explains Thibaut Fontaine, the head of France's anti-organised-crime unit.

Their lack of criminal experience, he says, makes them easier to arrest quickly.

Karine still has no idea why her son, Hugo, was shot.

Asked what she would say to gang leaders recruiting children and young men in Marseille, she says: "They destroy families. Little sisters, little brothers, grandparents. But then, I don't think those people have a heart, really."

The restaurant where her son said he was going to work did exist, but staï¬ there told Karine they had never recruited anyone called Hugo.

The reason for his death almost certainly lies within Marseille's Quartier Nord.

Additional reporting by Marianne Baisnee, Lea Guedj and Paul Pradier
